Same Coeffect, Different Base: Connecting Two Dominant Approaches to Graded Types (with appendices)
Vilem Liepelt, Danielle Marshall, Dominic Orchard

Abstract
. Graded types provide a way to augment a type system with fine-grained information, e.g., to track side effects or context dependence and resource use (called coeffects ). Graded types for coeffects have found their way into languages such as Haskell, Idris, and Granule, enabling resourceful reasoning via coeffect analysis with varying levels of generality. Two separate lineages of graded coeffect system have emerged in the last decade: those in which coeffect annotations are pervasive, requiring annotations on function types (which we call graded-base ) and those in which coeffects are added by way of a graded modal type operator atop linear types (which we call linear-base ). The latter has its origins in Girard’s Linear Logic which has been a rich humus for programming language research focused on resources, whereas the graded-base approach emerged in the mid-2010s, seeing rapid adoption in programming language theory and practice, e.g. in QTT and Linear Haskell. The relationship between these two styles has however remained an open question. We answer this question by giving translations between pairs of calculi of both lineages that we prove type-, grade- and operational-semantics preserving. We show that the same notions of context dependence can be expressed in either style, building a bridge between the two lineages that enables transfer of results and ideas, while helping language designers to make better informed choices.



\declaretheorem

[name=Theorem,style=definition]thrm

\declaretheorem
[name=Lemma,style=definition]lemm

Extended Version:

The official version of this paper (
doi.org/10.1145/3828697
) appears in the proceedings of the
2026 ACM SIGPLAN International Conference on Functional Programming (ICFP 2026).
The appendices included herein provide proofs and definitions omitted due to space constraints.

1.
Introduction

The behaviour of useful programs often depends on execution context—whether through low-level factors like
hardware capabilities (e.g., GPS sensors) or higher-level properties like information flow security.
Recent work has explored tracking such
coeffects
via the type
system
(
39
;
40
;
12
;
18
)
,
providing an analysis of program coeffects, reified into the type system as an intrinsic part of the
language’s meaning.

There exist in the literature two parallel threads on coeffect types systems: those which build atop
Linear Logic via the linear
\lambda
-calculus, which we call
linear-base
systems, and those
which build atop the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus or System F but with pervasive annotations on
all assumptions and function arrows, which we refer to as
graded-base
systems (for reasons which will
become clear later). How these two approaches precisely relate is a long-standing open question that this paper
answers, giving type system and language designers a map of the graded type system landscape.

Generalising linearity to graded modal types

21
introduced Linear Logic, seeking
to achieve more fine-grained control over the structural rules
implicitly present in classical and intuitionistic logic. The relevance to computer science in
particular was very much at the forefront of the motivations for
21
: “One of the main outputs of
Linear Logic seems to be in computer science”.

The more conscious use of structural properties in Linear Logic allows for reasoning about the cost
of computations and other properties, which are referred to under the umbrella term

resources
. As predicted by Girard himself, Linear Logic has become the mother to a profusion
of type systems for reasoning about various notions of resource
(
47
)
. Examples in the literature
include access to hardware devices
(
39
)
, timing
(
18
)
and differential privacy

(
15
)
. These developments stand on the shoulders of a refinement of Linear
Logic, in the form of Bounded Linear Logic (BLL), due to
20
in
the early 1990s. While Linear Logic delineates the world into ‘use strictly once’ (
A
) and
‘arbitrary usage’ (
!A
), i.e. zero or more times, BLL indexes the
of course
modality (‘bang’) with a
polynomial bound, where
!_{n}A
is usable up to
n
times.
For example, the following type describes some higher-order function which
takes a function and composes it with itself, thus using it twice:

!_{2}(A\multimap A)\multimap(A\multimap A)
.

The next significant evolution came in 2014, when
12
and
18

independently generalised the natural number indices in BLL to
elements of a semiring
\mathcal{R}
to capture more properties than
just variable (re)use, including privacy levels and hardware schedules.
This development then allowed other strands of research on
coeffects
(general
context analysis) to be expressible in this linear-logic-derived setting.

The generalisation of BLL gave rise to a more general paradigm
of
graded modal types
.
16
considered a calculus
with a semiring-graded modality
\Box_{r}A
for coeffects alongside a
monoid-graded modality for effects
\Diamond_{f}A
, modelled
respectively by graded comonads and graded
monads.
38
crystallised the idea of
graded modal types as one in which “graded modal types carry
information about semantic structure” where the algebraic structure
of the indices reflects underlying program
structure. Others
have followed a similar approach
(
48
;
49
;
25
;
23
)
.
In all these systems, Linear Logic has been at the core. We thus refer to
these as
linear-base systems
.

Coeffect systems with pervasive grading and without inherent linearity

In parallel,
39
;
40
dualised type-and-effect
systems to
coeffect systems
, capturing how programs depend on their
context.
Their approach built on top of the simply-typed
rather than the linear
\lambda
-calculus. Every assumption
has an associated coeffect (grade) and function arrows must also
be annotated with a coeffect describing how the parameter is used.
In the case of the ‘apply twice’ higher-order function,
the analogous coeffect type is
(A\xrightarrow{1}A)\xrightarrow{2}(A\xrightarrow{1}A)
. If the parameter function
is generalised from coeffect
1
to
some arbitrary use
r
, then the type would be

(A\xrightarrow{r}A)\xrightarrow{2}(A\xrightarrow{r*r}A)
.

Coeffect systems were noted by
12
to relate to a generalised
BLL system. Since then, many other graded
systems have taken a similar approach with no base notion
of linearity, but where every assumption has a grade and function arrows
are annotated, e.g., Linear Haskell (GHC/Haskell with the
LinearTypes

extension)
(
5
)
,
the general system of
1
,
Quantitative Type Theory, first due to
31
then refined
by
3
and used as the basis for Idris 2
(
11
)
, the
GraD
calculus
of
13
, Graded Modal Type Theory
(
34
)
,
and various others
(
27
;
24
;
2
)
. This style has even
been adapted to an imperative setting in the coeffectful Multi-Graded Featherweight Java
(
6
;
7
)
. In these
approaches, grading is pervasive and there is no inherent notion of linearity as in Linear Logic
(although linearity can be achieved as an emergent phenomenon). We refer to these as

graded-base systems
.

So what is the relationship? Are graded coeffects with a linear basis
equally expressive as those without an inherent notion of linearity? This is the open question
that has become ever more glaring as these works have developed. We need a clear understanding of their
relationship and relative expressivity in order to inform future developments
and choices.

Figure 1.
Relationship between graded calculi studied in this paper. For each calculus, a
selection of representative papers and programming systems is given (that typically also extend the
given calculus with orthogonal features, such as dependent types, which are not relevant to this paper). A dashed arrow denotes a
subset relationship. A solid arrow denotes a semantics-preserving translation which we
define in the present paper. A crossed-out arrow with a dotted shaft refers to a translation that
is not possible.

Contributions and roadmap

The main contribution of this paper is to elucidate the connection between two distinct strands of
coeffect reasoning that have emerged in the literature and in programming language
implementations, which we call
linear-base
vs.
graded-base
systems.
We show that, depending on the substrate, both systems can be given a mutual translations to each other in a sound way.

Figure
1

gives an overview of these two strands, instantiated as several concrete calculi with differing
features which we summarise here with relevant section numbers:

We have the linear-base systems on the left. Here
Linear Base
(
2.1
) represents a linear

\lambda
-calculus extended with graded modal necessity
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}
, corresponding to a
generalised
of course
modality.
Linear Core
(
5.1
) augments this with products and sums
(multiplicative conjunction and additive disjunction).
Linear Push Core
(
5.5
) further adds constructs for
allowing distributivity of graded modalities over products and sums, thereby losing the
correspondence to Linear Logic, as shown by
22
. This system can model the
interaction between grades and products/sums in Granule as presented by

38
.

On the right then we have the graded-base systems, for all of which
Graded Base
(
2.2
) is the
underlying calculus, corresponding to pervasively-graded simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus in the
style of
40
,
18
, and Linear Haskell as presented by

5
.
Graded Poly Base
(
4.1
) augments this with
System-F-style type abstraction as in the work of
1
, presenting a
substrate modelling more closely the actual implementation of Linear Haskell, as well as Idris 2
(where the dependent types have no interaction with grades)
(
11
)
and also
Granule with the
GradedBase
language extension
(
24
)
.

Graded Modal Base
(
3.1
) instead augments Graded Base by adding a graded modal necessity akin to the
one in Linear Base.
Graded Modal Core
(
5.2
) augments the latter system with products and sums and
thereby captures the interaction between grades and algebraic datatypes in Granule with the

GradedBase
extension but also Linear Haskell and Idris 2, where a modal operator can be
encoded via an algebraic datatype.

The paper is then structured as follows:

•

Section
2
introduces Linear Base and Graded Base. Section
2.3

introduces syntax for translation, gives a
translation from Graded Base to Linear Base

(
2.3.1
) with soundness results, and then
the converse
from Linear Base to Graded Base
via a CPS-like translation
(
2.3.2
).

•

Section
3
extends Graded Base to Graded Modal Base
(
3.1
), gives a
translation from Linear Base to Graded Modal Base

(
3.2.1
) with soundness results, gives a
translation from Graded Modal
Base to Linear Base
(
3.2.3
) with soundness results, investigates the nature of
the relationship between both translations (
3.2.4
), and gives a possible
generalisation of elimination for the graded modality that appears in the literature, along with tradeoffs
(
3.3
).

•

Section
4
gives an alternative strategy for adding a graded modality to Graded Base,
via an encoding inspired by Linear Haskell, extends Graded Base to Graded Poly Base
(
4.1
), and gives a
translation from Graded Poly Base to Linear Base

(
4.1.1
) with soundness results.

•

Section
5
adds product and sum types, allowing us to study the interaction of
coeffects with data structures. It extends Linear Base to Linear Core (
5.1
), extends
Graded Modal Base to Graded Modal Core (
5.2
), gives a
translation from
Linear Core to Graded Modal Core
(
5.3
) with soundness results, shows

there is no sound translation from Graded Modal Core to Linear Core

(
5.4
), extends Linear Core to Linear Push Core
(
5.5
), and gives a
translation from Graded Modal
Core to Linear Push Core
with soundness results.

•

Section
6
asks
Is one approach better than the other?

(
6.1
); gives some simple example programs in languages that implement features from the
two lineages studied (
6.2
); considers some related ideas (
6.3
-
6.6
);
and concludes (
6.7
).

2.
The Two Calculi: Linear and Graded Base

We present in turn two representative calculi for
the two dominant styles of graded coeffects:
Linear Base (Section
2.1
)
Graded Base (Section
2.2
).

2.1.
Linear Base

As discussed in the introduction, linear-base systems take the
linear
\lambda
-calculus as their core. Such systems include
the core quantitative coeffect calculus of
12
,
and the combined graded system for effects and coeffects of
16
,

\lambda\mathcal{R}
of
48
,
the core of Granule by
38
,
and associated work
(
25
)
.

The term syntax of our idealised system Linear Base is as follows:

(terms)

\displaystyle\mathit{t}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\mid\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

Terms comprise variables, applications, and abstraction as standard. Graded modal introduction
(formally
promotion
, informally
boxing
) is via
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}
, which for now we can
think of as wrapping a term in a constructor. Graded modal elimination eliminates this constructor
via a
let
binding, unwrapping the boxed value, which we refer to as
unboxing
.

Linear Base types include a linear function space, a

graded modality
which can be understood as generalising Linear Logic’s exponential
modality
!
, and a base type
\mathrm{K}
so that types are well-founded:

(types)

\displaystyle\mathit{A}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\mid\mathrm{K}

Here
\mathit{r}
is drawn from a pre-ordered
semiring
(\mathcal{R},\cdot,1,+,0,\sqsubseteq)
parameterising the calculus.

An intuition for
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}
is that it describes some capability
r

with which terms of type
A
contained inside can be used. For example,
for the natural numbers semiring
\mathbb{N}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{2}}\mathit{A}
denotes two copies
of an
A
, or in essence that its contents can be used twice.

Typing judgments are of the form
\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
, relating a context
\Gamma
, term
\mathit{t}
, and type
\mathit{A}
. Contexts comprise linear assumptions

\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}
and graded assumptions
\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}

with grade
\mathit{r}\in\mathcal{R}
, of the form:

(contexts)

\displaystyle\Gamma::=\emptyset\mid\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\mid\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}

Contexts can be
added
if they are disjoint in their linear assumptions and
using semiring addition to combine grades of any graded assumptions shared
between two contexts:

Definition 2.1 (Context addition).

For contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is a partial operation
computing the combined
context providing
contraction
on graded assumptions. Context addition is specified:

\displaystyle\Gamma+\emptyset\triangleq\Gamma\quad(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}})+(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{s}}}})

\displaystyle\triangleq(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}),\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}+\mathit{s}}}}

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}})+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}),\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{if}\ x\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A})+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}),\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\mathrm{if}\ x\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})

That is, we union the disjoint parts of the contexts, and for the non-disjoint parts
of the contexts we merge with semiring addition. If the two contexts share linear assumptions
or if the two contexts share graded assumptions that do not agree on the type, then
the addition is undefined (in practical terms, this amounts to a linearity error).
Contexts are unordered, i.e.,
exchange
is permitted.

We define two predicates for denoting contexts containing only graded assumptions
and those containing graded assumptions all with the same grade:

Definition 2.2 (Graded contexts).

A context
\Gamma
has property
\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma)
if it contains only graded
assumptions, and
\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}})
if it contains only graded assumptions which are
of grade
\mathit{r}
. These predicates are defined inductively:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathrm{graded}(\emptyset)}\;\textsc{{}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma)\\
\end{array}}{\mathrm{graded}((\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}))}\;\textsc{{}{}}}\qquad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathrm{graded}(\emptyset,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}})}\;\textsc{{}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}})\\
\end{array}}{\mathrm{graded}((\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}),{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}})}\;\textsc{{}{}}}\end{array}

Lastly, contexts which contain only graded assumptions can be
scaled
by
a grade:

Definition 2.3 (Scalar multiplication).

A context
\Gamma
where
\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma)
can be
scaled

{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Gamma
, defined:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\emptyset\triangleq\emptyset\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{s}}}})\triangleq({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}),\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}\cdot\mathit{s}}}}

\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
Typing rules

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{var${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{abs${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{app${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\\[12.50002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}&\;\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}0})\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{weak${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{der${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}&\;\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma)\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{pr${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\\[12.50002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{let${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\qquad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{B}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}&\;\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\mathit{s}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{B}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{s}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{approx${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

Figure 2.
Linear Base typing.

Figure
2
defines typing rules. The
variable

(
var
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) and
abstraction
(
abs
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) rules are standard for the
linear
\lambda
-calculus. The
application
(
app
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) rule is close to that of

\lambda
-calculus, but the separate contexts of the premises are added in the conclusion.

Weakening
(
weak
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) allows unused assumptions to be added, as long as they
are graded at the zero of the semiring.
Dereliction
(
der
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) treats a linear
assumption as a graded assumption at the one of the semiring, which connects the linear and
non-linear worlds.
Promotion
(
pr
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) introduces grading to terms, making
use of scalar context multiplication to propagate the resource requirements to the assumptions. The

let
or
unboxing
rule (
let
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) eliminates an
\mathit{r}
-graded
modality, making
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
available in the body of
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
according to the capabilities
afforded by
\mathit{r}
.
Approximation
(
approx
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) allows treating an
\mathit{r}
-graded assumption as
\mathit{s}
-graded if
\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\mathit{s}
. Note the direction of the graded
inequality in the premise is according to
40
. The opposite polarity is
also used in the literature, for example by
12
, without any semantic difference.

Example 2.4.

The natural numbers semiring
(\mathbb{N},\ast,1,+,0,\equiv)
whose ordering
is equality (i.e., a discrete ordering) captures a notion of exact usage.
For example, the following captures the typing of a linear function
f
that
takes two inputs of type
\mathit{A}
, which are both provided here by
\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{2}}\mathit{A}
.
This is first eliminated into a graded assumption
\mathit{y}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{2}}}
which is then
used twice to apply
f
:

\displaystyle\frac{\ldots\vskip-10.00002pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{f}\,\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right={app${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\mathit{y}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{f}\,\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={der${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}\,\frac{\frac{\hskip 7.3612pt}{\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right={var${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{y}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={der${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}\hskip-16.38895pt}{\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\mathit{y}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{f}\,\mathit{y}\,\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}\hskip-49.16684pt}\quad\textsc{right={app${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}\hskip-50.00008pt}{\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{2}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{f}\,\mathit{y}\,\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{right={let${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}

Example 2.5 (None-one-tons
(
31
;
3
)
, a.k.a. Linearity Semiring).

Linearity can be captured with the semiring
\mathcal{R}=\{0,1,\omega\}
, with the required laws for
0
and
1
, and
1+1=\omega
and
\omega+\mathit{r}=\omega=\mathit{r}+\omega
and
\omega\cdot\omega=\omega
, and ordering
0\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\omega
and
1\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\omega
. This captures linear reasoning in a graded modal setting
(
1
;
2
)
,
with
1
denoting linearity and
\omega
for
non-linearity, and
0
providing a slightly finer-grained
view when non-linearly discarding.

Example 2.6 (Security
(
1
;
16
;
27
)
).

Consider a semi-lattice of security levels
\mathcal{L}=\{\mathsf{Lo},\mathsf{Hi}\}
with pre-ordering
\mathsf{Lo}\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Hi}
.
This forms a semiring with multiplication as join
\cdot=\vee

with unit
1=\mathsf{Lo}
, and addition as meet
+=\wedge

with unit
0=\mathsf{Hi}
. We can type a function which maps
low-security to high-security values, using approximation with the
flipped
\sqsupseteq
ordering in the Linear Base calculus:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\hskip 7.3612pt}{\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right={var${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathsf{Lo}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={der${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathsf{Hi}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathsf{Hi}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{right={pr${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathsf{Lo}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathsf{Hi}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{right={approx${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}

A function mapping from
\mathsf{Hi}
to
\mathsf{Lo}
values is derivable but cannot
use its input. This
non-interference
property is proved by

1
and
27
.

Example 2.7 (Product).

Given two pre-ordered semirings
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
,
their product
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{S}
is a semiring, with
zero element
\langle 0,0\rangle\in(\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{S})
,
one element
\langle 1,1\rangle\in(\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{S})
, and
operations and ordering acting pointwise. This is a standard
algebraic construction which has been used in graded systems to combine
analyses (e.g.
(
38
)
).

Section
3.2.2
uses this construction to
compose the linearity semiring (Example
2.5
) with some other
given semiring, thereby making explicit the linearity analysis inherent to Linear Base.

7
present the
smash product
construction
as a a variation on the product construction which has some preconditions and replaces
elements of the cartesian product having the left or the right
=0
,
instead collapsing them into a canonical
\hat{0}
element.

Lemma 2.8 (Admissibility of substitution).

There are two admissible substitutions:

(1)

(Linear) If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}

then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}
;

(2)

(Graded) If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\mathrm{graded}(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}})
and

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}

then
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}
.

Remark 2.9.

In the literature (e.g.
12
;
16
;
38
),
the graded context predicates (
2.2
) are often presented instead as partial identity functions on contexts:
written
[\Gamma]
, the function is identity when the context contains only
graded assumptions and is undefined otherwise, preventing a rule from
being applied (and similarly
[\Gamma]_{r}
for contexts
containing only assumptions graded by
r
).
We chose to make the predicates explicit for clarity.

Operational Semantics

Figure
3
(first group) gives the
operational semantics, which is that of the call-by-name

\lambda
-calculus with an additional congruence rule
for graded modal elimination and
\beta
-reduction of graded modal
introduction followed by elimination.

Note that all the operational semantics we consider in this paper represent the standard
models given in the literature in question, with progress and
preservation theorems, but these do not track specific resource
behaviour operationally (see discussion of resource-aware operational
models in Section
6.6
).

Appendix
A
gives the definition of syntactic
substitution which is standard.

Equational theory

Figure
3
(second group) defines an equational theory, giving the key rules.

\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\subseteq\equiv_{\textsc{l}}
.

Definition 2.10 (Freshness).

We make use of freshness here and throughout. It is defined as follows:

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\#t\triangleq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cap\mathsf{fv}(t)=\emptyset

2.2.
Graded Base

The next calculus, Graded Base, is representative of the coeffect systems of

39
;
40
,
the generalised bounded linear type system of
18
,
and the OO-based approach of
6
.
The syntax of terms is that of the
\lambda
-calculus:

(terms)

\displaystyle\mathit{t}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}

We use
t
to also range over Graded Base terms but disambiguate which
calculus terms belong to via the context of their later use (in translations).
Type comprise graded functions and a base type
\mathrm{K}
:

(types)

\displaystyle\mathit{A}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}\mid\mathrm{K}

(contexts)

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle::=\emptyset\mid\Delta,\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}

Instead of a linear function type and graded modality, Graded Base has just a
graded function type
, where
r\in\mathcal{R}
. Contexts contain only graded assumptions which we instead denote as
\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}
.

Figure
4
(first group) gives the typing with judgments of the form
\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
. Note the singleton context graded at
1

in the
variable
rule. The
abstraction
rule introduces its binding into the context
graded at
\mathit{r}
, per the graded function arrow. In the
application
rule, the function
arrow propagates the function’s resource requirements onto its argument
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
via scalar
multiplication on
\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}
(i.e., the context of
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
). Weakening scales by
0
all the grades in an additional context
\Delta^{\prime}
.

\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
Typing rules

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}1}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{var${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}0\cdot}\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{weak${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta,\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}&\;\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\mathit{s}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta,\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{s}}}\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{approx${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\\[12.50002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta,\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{abs${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}&\;\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{app${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\end{array}

\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}
Operational semantics

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\beta_{\textsf{G}}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congApp}_{\textsf{G}}$}{}}}\end{array}

\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}
Equational theory (congruence rules omitted)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{g}-\beta}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}\,\#\,\mathit{t}\\
