\displaystyle\;\triangleq\;({\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi_{{\mathrm{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\text{@}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{B}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis})\,(\lambda\mathit{x}.{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi_{{\mathrm{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis})

The rest of the translation follows the structure of the previous term
translation.

In the following, we write
\Pi:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}

to mean that
\Pi
is the derivation of a judgment
\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
.

{thrm}

[][Soundness of the Linear Base to Graded Poly Base translation]

•

Type preservation:
\Pi:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\vdash_{\textsc{g}_{\forall}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}:{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}

•

Operational correspondence:
(\Pi:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A})\wedge(\Pi^{\prime}:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}:\mathit{A})\wedge(\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}_{\forall}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}

•

Equation preservation:
(\Pi:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A})\wedge(\Pi^{\prime}:\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}:\mathit{A})\wedge(\mathit{t}\equiv^{-\eta\Box}_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\equiv_{\textsc{g}_{\forall}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Pi^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}

Appendix
B.6
provides the proofs.

Note the operational correspondence
needs to have typing derivations of the source term and target term.
Furthermore, the translation does not preserve
\eta
-equality for the graded modality; only the
\beta
-equality rules, commuting conversions, and congruence rules are preserved;
we thus denote the subset of the preserved equational theory here as
\equiv^{-\eta\Box}_{\textsc{l}}
.
This is because the translation of a term
t
of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}

may not be of the form
\Lambda\beta.\lambda\mathit{f}.\mathit{f}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}
for some
t^{\prime}
(e.g., it could be just a free variable), which is required for
\eta
-equality to apply.
Instead, parametricity reasoning (based on the polymorphic typing) would provide that
t
is
observationally equivalent
to
\Lambda\beta.\lambda\mathit{f}.\mathit{f}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}
for some
t^{\prime}
,
and thus the translation preserves
\eta
-equality up to observational equivalence, though not up to syntactic equality;
we leave the details for further work.

Remark 4.1.

(
Ur
is not the
!
-modality)

5
suggest
Ur
as
an encoding of Linear Logic’s
!
-modality, although
22
later show that this
encoding in Linear Haskell and similarly in Idris 2, as well as the graded modality in Granule, do not exactly correspond to Linear Logic’s
!
-modality. Once (multiplicative) products
are included, Linear Logic does not admit distributing
!
over products, i.e.,

!(A\otimes B)\multimap\,!A\,\otimes\,!B
is not derivable. However the aforementioned systems permit its derivation due to the interaction between graded modalities and products
(and similarly sums). We consider calculi with products and sums next
as this takes us closer towards practical programming and it allows us to uncover
other expressivity differences in the literature.

5.
Extending Linear Base and Graded Modal Base with Products and Sums

Thus far the calculi studied have only covered the implicational
fragments of the two approaches, i.e., just function types and
variables. Adding product and sum types to the calculi brings the
theory closer to a useful basis for understanding actual
programming languages such as the linear-types extension to Haskell (graded-base) or Granule
(linear-base).
We thus now add products and sums to Linear Base and Graded Modal Base
following standard approaches in the literature for both flavours
of linear-base and graded-base systems. This raises interesting questions
that delineate the expressive power of linear- vs. graded-base
calculi. From linear-base to graded-base, a straightforward
homomorphic translation is possible. However, for the opposite
direction we will see a difference in expressivity that needs handling
by extending the linear-base system.

Syntax

We extend Linear Base and Graded Modal Base (which already share the same
syntax) with the same extended syntax:

\displaystyle\mathit{t}

\displaystyle::=...\mid\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\mid\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mid\langle\rangle\mid\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

(terms)

\displaystyle\mid

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}\mid\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}\mid\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}

These additional term formers provide product introduction and elimination, unit introduction
and elimination, and sum (coproduct) introduction and elimination respectively.

The operational semantics are the standard call-by-name operational semantics for products and sums given in Figure
5
for Linear Core, with the same
added to operational semantics of Graded Modal Core. We elide the
\beta\eta
-equational theory which is standard (see Appendices
A.2
and

A.7
).

\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}
Standard operational semantics (Call-By-Name) for products, units, and sums

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congProd}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}[\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\beta_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congUnit}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\langle\rangle\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\beta\textsc{Unit}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congCase}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathsf{case}\,\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{caseInj1}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathsf{case}\,\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{caseInj2}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\end{array}

\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
Typing rules

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$\otimes$-i${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}}\;\textsc{{{$\otimes$-e${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\emptyset\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\langle\rangle:\mathrm{unit}}\;\textsc{{{$\mathbf{1}$-i${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathrm{unit}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{$\mathbf{1}$-e${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$\oplus$-i${}_{1\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$\oplus$-i${}_{2\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}\end{array}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}:\mathit{C}}\;\textsc{{{$\oplus$-e${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

Figure 5.
Linear Core semantics and typing

To type the new syntax, we extend the type system of Linear Base to a
system we call
Linear Core

(Section
5.1
), and extend Graded Modal Base to

Graded Modal Core
(Section
5.2
).
Section
5.3
then gives the
translation from Linear Core to Graded Modal Core which is
straightforwardly homomorphic.
Section
5.4
then
shows that the opposite direction is not possible, requiring an
extension of Linear Core to
Linear Push Core
in
Section
5.5
, at the cost of weakening the correspondence between our
\Box
-modality
and Linear Logic’s
!
-modality. We then give the translation
from Graded Modal Core to Linear Push Core
(Section
5.6
) and the translation
from Linear Push Core to Graded Modal Core
(Section
5.7
).

5.1.
Linear Core

Linear Base augmented with products and sums becomes what we call
Linear Core
.
Linear Core features the following new type constructors for products,
units, and sums:

(types)

\displaystyle\mathit{A}

\displaystyle::=...\mid\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}\mid\mathrm{unit}\mid\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}

Typing

Linear Core extends Linear Base typing (given in Figure
2
)
with the judgments for multiplicative products and its unit, and additive disjunction, in Figure
5
, which
are standard from Linear Logic
(
21
)
. This is the same typing as in linear-base systems such as Granule
(
38
;
25
)
, the synthesis calculus of
22
, and that of
12
(although
their system has a less general natural numbers data type instead of products and sums, the same insights apply).

As in Linear Base, any terms comprising multiple sub-terms have their contexts
combined using context addition
+
which adds together the grades of
graded assumptions using semiring addition.

5.2.
Graded Modal Core

Graded Modal Base augmented with products and sums becomes
Graded Modal Core
.
Graded Modal Core features the following new type constructors for products, units, and sums:

(types)

\displaystyle\mathit{A}

\displaystyle::=...\mid\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}\mid\mathrm{unit}\mid\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}

Typing

Graded Modal Core extends Graded Modal Base typing (given in Section
3.1
) by the judgments
given in Figure
6
. The typing is akin to that shown
by
1
, in Linear Haskell
(
5
)
, and by
13
(although they restrict their
products initially to
r=1
).

In the elimination of the products we may use each variable
according to grade
\mathit{r}
and thus the context
\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}
for the eliminated product
is multiplied by
\mathit{r}
in the conclusion. Thus, the usage of the components is propagated
to demands on the dependencies for the product. The elimination of sums
has a similar idea, propagating demands on the variables
x
and
y
through to the
dependencies of the eliminated sum term
t
. There is an additional side condition here too

\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,1
accounting for usage incurred by the inspection of the sum
constructor, and thus some information gained. This side condition is key to applications of
graded coeffect types for information-flow control
(
1
;
27
)
:
if the grade here is
r=\mathsf{Hi}
, representing
a high-security value then the side condition fails as
1=\mathsf{Lo}
and
thus
\mathsf{Hi}\not\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Lo}
. This prevents pattern matching on a high-security
value as a control-flow attack to leak information. Lastly note the elimination of
the unit type similarly allows (an arbitrary) scaling of the scrutinee’s context, inline with
presentations in the literature (e.g.,
(
1
)
).

\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
Typing rules

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}&\;\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle:\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$\times$-i${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}&\;\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}}\;\textsc{{{$\times$-e${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\\[12.50002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\emptyset\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\langle\rangle:\mathrm{unit}}\;\textsc{{{$\mathsf{unit}$-i${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathrm{unit}&\;\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}}\;\textsc{{{$\mathsf{unit}$-e${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$+$-i${}_{1\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\\[12.50002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{B}\\
\end{array}}{\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$+$-i${}_{2\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}\quad\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{C}\quad\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{y}:_{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}\quad\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,1\\
\end{array}}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}\cdot}\Delta_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Delta_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}:\mathit{C}}\;\textsc{{{$+$-e${}_{\textsf{G}}$}}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

Figure 6.
Graded Modal Core additional typing rules.

5.3.
Translation from Linear Core to Graded Modal Core

We extend the translation from Section
3.2.1
via a homomorphism
on the additional syntax:

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\hspace{0em}}r}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis},{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\rangle\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle={\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\langle\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\langle\rangle\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle={\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt&\text{(Term translation)}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{case}\,{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\[5.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\times{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{B}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}+{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{B}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\mathrm{unit}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}&\triangleq&\;\mathrm{unit}\hfil\hskip 0.0pt&\text{(Type translation)}\end{array}}

We can then re-establish the two key results about the translation (proofs in
Appendix
B.8
):

{thrm}
[][Soundness of the Linear Core to Graded Modal Core translation]

•

Type preservation:
\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}:{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
;

•

Operational correspondence:
\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t^{\prime}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
;

•

Equation preservation:
\mathit{t}\,\equiv_{\textsc{l}}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t^{\prime}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
.

A notable point of the type preservation proof is that when translating sum elimination from Linear Core to
Graded Modal Core, we pick the grade
r=1
which satisfies the side condition that
1\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,1

trivially by reflexivity of the pre-order.

5.4.
No Type-Sound Translation from Graded Modal Core to Linear Core

We now attempt a similar homomorphic translation (
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}-{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}
) back from Graded Modal Core
to Linear Core, focusing on just products and sums:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq\langle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ},{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\rangle\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle={\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathsf{inj}_{i}\,\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq\mathsf{inj}_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq\mathsf{case}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}\end{array}

However, this translation is unsound: consider the following Graded Modal Core terms,
f
and
g
,
which manifest a distributivity of grading over products and sums:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rl}f&:(\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B})\\
f&=\lambda\mathit{p}.(\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{p}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle)\end{array}\begin{array}[]{rl}g&:(\mathit{A}+\mathit{B})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}+{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B})\quad(\text{where }\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,1)\\
g&=\lambda\mathit{e}.\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{e}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\}\end{array}

Thus
f
is a function that distributes the resource requirements on a pair onto its components, and similarly,
g
distributes the resource requirements on a sum onto the components of its injections.

Applying the above translation to
f
and
g
yields the following ill-typed terms
(to avoid clutter, we consider
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}
interchangeable with
\mathit{A}
):

(ill-typed)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{f}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}(\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B})\multimap({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{f}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathit{b}.(\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{p}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{b}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{p}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{g}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}(\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B})\multimap({\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{g}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathit{b}.(\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{e}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{b}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{e}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\})

To see why these are ill-typed, the following gives a partial derivation for the body of

{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{f}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\mathit{p}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{p}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right={var${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}\hskip 36.80603ptr=1}{\mathit{p}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{p}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={der${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}*}\hskip 32.7779pt\frac{\frac{}{\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=?}\hskip 29.44482pt\frac{}{\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=?}}{\mathit{x}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}+\mathit{y}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={$\otimes$-i${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{\mathit{p}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]_{\mathit{r}}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{p}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{Right={$\otimes$-e${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}}{{\mathit{b}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}(\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B})\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{p}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{b}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{p}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}}\quad\textsc{Right={let${}_{\textsf{L}}$}{}}

At the points marked with question marks (?), we cannot apply promotion (
pr
{}_{\textsf{L}}
) to

x
and
y
, because the
graded
predicate in the
antecedent does not hold; they are linear variables. Further, to apply
der
{}_{\textsf{L}}
at
the point marked with an asterisk (*), we would have to restrict to
r=1
.
A similar attempted derivation for
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{g}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}_{\circ}
, commuting the graded modality
and sum types, encounters the same issues.

One may wonder if any sound translation is possible at all.
22
have explored
precisely this point and show that linear-base and graded-base systems exhibit a fundamental
difference in expressivity. Linear Core is akin to Linear Logic, in that it does
not admit derivation of the distributive law
!(A\otimes B)\multimap\,!A\otimes!B
as the
semantics of multiplicative tensor
\otimes
is that each component must be used equally; such a
distributive law would allow
A
and
B
to be decoupled and used differently. On the other hand,
Graded Base and the graded-base systems in the literature cited above admit such a distributive law
as seen with
f
. Thus Graded Base cannot model Linear Logic’s
!
-modality exactly through
any

choice of semiring for the graded modality.

22
propose extensions to graded systems to bring back the restrictions of
Linear Logic. Here we explore a different route, namely we extend Linear Core by further constructs,
which admit the augmented expressivity of Graded Modal Core, giving the system
Linear Push Core
.

5.5.
Linear Push Core

We extend Linear Core to
Linear Push Core
with new syntax, heaving it to a level of
expressivity on par with Graded Modal Core, leaving behind the restrictions of Linear Logic
discussed in
5.4
.

Since the essential missing piece is the distributive laws between the graded modality and
products and sums, we complete the syntax of Linear Push Core with the following two constructs:

(terms)

\displaystyle\mathit{t}

\displaystyle::=...\mid\textsf{push}_{\otimes}\mathit{t}\mid\textsf{push}_{\oplus}\mathit{t}\mid\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}\mathit{t}

The syntax is non-standard but isomorphic to other approaches; Granule achieves the same
expressivity implicitly via pattern matching
(
38
)
, but
we prefer to introduce explicit syntax here. These two constructs provide exactly
the distributive behaviour captured in product and sum elimination in Graded Modal Core.
Figure
7
gives the typing and operational model.

\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}
Typing rules

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}(\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B})\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\otimes}\mathit{t}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{push${}_{\otimes}$}{}}}\,{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathit{r}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,1&\;\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}(\mathit{A}\oplus\mathit{B})\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\oplus}\mathit{t}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{push${}_{\oplus}$}{}}}\,{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathrm{unit}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}\mathit{t}:\mathrm{unit}}\;\textsc{{push${}_{\mathsf{unit}}$}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}
Operational semantics for push constructors

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\otimes}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle}\;\textsc{{$\beta\textsc{Push}_{\otimes\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\langle\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\langle\rangle}\;\textsc{{$\beta\textsc{Push}_{\mathsf{unit}\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathsf{inj}_{i}\,\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{inj}_{i}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}}\;\textsc{{$\beta\textsc{push}_{\oplus_{i}\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[10.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\otimes}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\otimes}\mathit{t^{\prime}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congPush}_{\otimes\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\oplus}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\oplus}\mathit{t^{\prime}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congPush}_{\oplus\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congPush}_{\mathsf{unit}\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\\[10.00002pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\otimes}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\otimes}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congPush}^{\Box}_{\otimes\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\

\end{array}}{\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{t^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}}\;\textsc{{$\textsc{congPush}^{\Box}_{\mathsf{unit}\textsf{L}}$}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

Figure 7.
Linear Push Core

5.6.
Translation from Graded Modal Core to Linear Push Core

We define the translation
from Graded Modal Core into Linear Push Core as follows
(fixing the unsound translation to Linear Core from Section
5.4

in the product and sum elimination):

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\hspace{-4em}}r}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\langle{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket},{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\rangle\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x^{\prime}},\mathit{y^{\prime}}\rangle=\textsf{push}_{\otimes}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{x^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{y^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\\
\lx@intercol\hskip 1.99997pt\;\quad\triangleq\;\mathsf{case}\,\textsf{push}_{\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{x^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{y^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\langle\rangle\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\langle\rangle\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt&\text{(Term translation)}\\[5.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{B}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt\\
{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\hskip 0.7pt\mathrm{unit}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}&\triangleq&\;\mathrm{unit}\hfil\hskip-40.00006pt&\text{(Type translation)}\end{array}}

Translation of a product elimination on
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
first promotes
the product to a graded modality which is then ‘pushed into’ each component of the product via
\mathsf{push}
. The resulting graded modal components
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are then eliminated in the scope of
\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
. A similar idea is applied for sum elimination. Thus this translation
gets at the heart of what is happening inside Graded Modal Core (and why it differs from
Linear Core): its eliminators distribute the coeffect analysis inside products and sums,
which we now capture explicitly here in Linear Push Core.

We can now re-establish the core results (the proofs for which are
in Appendix
B.7
):

{thrm}
[][Soundness of the Graded Modal Core to Linear Push Core translation]

•

Type preservation:
\Delta\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}:{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{A}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}
;

•

Operational correspondence:
\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}
.

•

Equation preservation:
(\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}\equiv^{-\eta\otimes\oplus\mathsf{unit}}_{\textsc{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\llbracket}\mathit{t^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4922,0.3047,0.6016}\rrbracket}
.

Our translation does not, in general, preserve
\eta
-equalities for
products, sums, and unit (it can only preserve
\eta
on these types
for closed terms); i.e., our translation is not extensional due to
additional ‘push’-structure for handling nesting of graded modalities
and products, sums, and units.

Given that we have changed Linear Core to Linear Push Core by adding two new constructs,
we now re-establish that we can translate Linear Push Core back into Graded Modal Core.

5.7.
Translation from Linear Push Core to Graded Modal Core

We extend the Linear Core to Graded Modal Core translation with the following
additional cases to handle the extra constructs of Linear Push Core:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}{\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\textsf{push}_{\otimes}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\;\triangleq\;\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}={\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{y},\mathit{z}\rangle=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]},{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{z}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\rangle}}\\
{\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\textsf{push}_{\oplus}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\;\triangleq\;\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}={\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{z}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{z}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}\}}}\\
{\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\textsf{push}_{\mathrm{unit}}\mathit{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\;\triangleq\;\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{x}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}={\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{x}\,\mathsf{in}\,\langle\rangle}}\end{array}

Note that these are essentially the programs
f
and
g
shown in Section
5.4

which are the derivation of
push
combinators in Graded Modal Core.

We thus (re)establish the key results (proofs in Appendix
B.8
):

{thrm}

[][Soundness of the Linear Push Core to Graded Modal Core translation]

•

Type preservation:
\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{A}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\vdash_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}:{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{A}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
;

•

Operational correspondence:
\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t^{\prime}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
;

•

Equation preservation:
\mathit{t}\,\equiv_{\textsc{l}}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t^{\prime}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
.

Note that whilst the operational correspondence from Linear Base to Graded Modal Base mapped a single
reduction
\mathit{t}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}
to a single graded-base reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}\rightsquigarrow_{\textsc{g}}{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\llparenthesis}\hskip 0.7pt\mathit{t^{\prime}}\hskip 0.7pt{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4453,0.1016}\rrparenthesis}
this
is now not possible for Linear Push Core. Instead, a single reduction in Linear Push Core for
the
\mathsf{push}
combinators corresponds to several reductions in Graded Modal Core.

Remark 5.1.

Graded Modal Core extends Graded Modal Base rather than Graded Base.
If Graded Modal Core did not have a graded modality it could not give a precise type to projection on products:

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash_{\textsc{g}}(\lambda\mathit{z}.\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{x}):(\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}

where
1\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\mathit{r}
due to the use of
x
and
0\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\sqsubseteq}\,\mathit{r}
due to the
absence of
y
. For the exact-usage natural numbers semiring this
would not be typeable as there is no such
r
satisfying both
constraints. For a semiring of intervals denoting lower-bound and
upper-bound usage
(
38
)
, the
constraints would be satisfied by
r=[0,1]
.

We instead opted to include the graded modality in Graded Modal Core as in the work of
1
,
34
, and others
(
24
;
27
)
.
Thus we can specify for this example a more fine-grained type via the graded modality,
where the
\mathit{B}
component of the product is unused:

\emptyset\vdash_{\textsc{g}}\lambda\mathit{z}.\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{z}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{let}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}[}\mathit{y^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}]}=\mathit{y}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{x}:(\mathit{A}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{0}}\mathit{B})\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}1}}\mathit{A}

Without the inclusion of graded modalities, Graded Modal Core is less expressive than Linear Core.

Note that some dependently-typed graded-base systems such as
GraD

(
13
)

and QTT
(
3
)
omit the graded modality but include a graded
dependent
\Sigma
type which can be used to encode the graded modality.

6.
Discussion and Conclusion

6.1.
Is one approach better than the other?

A natural question at this point from the language designer’s perspective is: which approach to
linearity should I use in my language? We do not try to offer an authoritative answer, but instead give
some observations and points to consider.

Clearly, Graded-Base style approaches afford a simpler system for the end-user with less syntactic overhead. The
pervasive grading obviates the need for most promotions and
let
-unboxings.
But while having explicit
push
syntax is an additional burden to the user of a
linear-base system, in practice this is not necessary. For example, Granule handles this implicitly
via pattern matching
(
38
;
22
)
.

On the flip-side, it may be desirable to disallow
push
for reasoning about certain
domains, where a coeffect should not distribute over the components of a pair. An example for this
might be for reasoning about interdependent threads that need to run in parallel. Given what we
have shown in this paper, Linear Base would seem better equipped to offer this more restrictive
modality, however
22
have shown that it is possible to recover the non-distributive behaviour of the
\Box
-modality with respect to products in a graded-base setting.
They argue that any useful language implementation would reasonably have to offer both flavours of
the modality.

What about the burden for the language designer? Empirically, the authors have found that proofs for
graded-base systems are significantly shorter, due, in part, to the homogeneous treatment of
assumptions, i.e. the lack of split between linear and non-linear free variables. Especially
when retrofitting linear or coeffect types to an existing language, only Graded Base offers a
backwards-compatible way and in such settings this is the only practical option.

6.2.
Graded Type Systems in Practice

The
LinearTypes
language extension available since GHC 9.0.1
(
42
)

enables Linear Haskell in the context of an
industrial-strength programming language. As discussed in this paper, Linear Haskell is a graded-base system, which is currently semiring-monomorphic: in the theory
of
5
the
none-one-tons
semiring is used,

\{0,1,\omega\}
. However, as of the time of writing,
the implementation in GHC offers no
0
, and
thus the grades range over
\{1,\omega\}
, written in Haskell as
One
and
Many
, respectively.
Consider the following Linear Haskell program:

⬇

{-#

LANGUAGE

DataKinds

#-}

import

GHC
.
Types

const

::

a

%
One

->

b

%
Many

->

a

const

x

y

=

x

By default arrows are
Many
graded, thus the above can also be written
as:

⬇

const
’

::

a

%
One

->

b

->

a

const
’

x

y

=

x

Notably, Linear Haskell supports
multiplicity polymorphism
, however the multiplicities
are inhabited by a single semiring.
Future work would be a
GradedTypes
extension to generalise Haskell to be polymorphic over
semirings, a feature supported by Granule, where
38
use an
SMT-solver to discharge constraints over the grades. It is not yet clear how this could be
practically integrated with GHC, perhaps as a compiler plugin akin to Liquid Haskell
(
44
)
.

Idris 2 also implements a graded-base system, based on Quantitative
Type Theory (due initially to
31
and later adapted
slightly by
3
), also providing
grades in the
none-one-tons
semiring
(
11
;
10
)
. For example,
const
can be rendered in Idris 2:

⬇

const

x

y

=

x

Granule is a research language serving as a test bed for ideas
in graded types
(
38
)
. In our classification,
Granule is a linear-base system by default.

Semiring-graded modalities written here as
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}
in the
core calculi are written as ‘
a

[
r
]
’ in Granule.
While Granule uses
\rightarrow
for the function arrow, as in Haskell or ML, it is really a linear arrow.
Our running example looks as follows in Granule:

⬇

1

const

:

\forall

{
a

:

Type
,

b

:

Type
,

s

:

Semiring
}

.

a

\rightarrow

b

[
0

:

s
]

\rightarrow

a

2

const

x

y

=

let

[
_
]

=

y

in

x

Granule also includes a language extension that changes its semantics to Graded
Base and whose syntax for graded arrows is borrowed from Linear Haskell, but supporting
semiring polymorphism:

⬇

language

GradedBase

\forall

{

,

,

}

\rightarrow

(0

)

\rightarrow

a

3

const
’

x

=

6.3.
Comparison with Effects

Here we have considered the relationship between Linear Base
with linear function spaces and linear assumptions as well
as graded, and Graded Base where grades are pervasive. This
situation is somewhat analogous to the relationship between

effect systems
and
monads
.

The type-and-effect systems of Gifford and
Lucassen [
(
19
;
30
)
]
provide a technique for augmenting a type system with information
about the side effects a program may perform whilst computing
a result. Judgments and function arrows carry effect information:

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:\tau,F\qquad\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda x.M^{\prime}:\sigma\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\tau,\emptyset

i.e., the program
M
computes a result of type
\tau
and may perform side effects described by

F
. Traditionally
F
is a set of effect ‘labels’, describing
operations that have been performed. This was later generalised to
semilattices and ordered
monoids
(
36
;
35
)
,
enabling other kinds of analysis. On the right,
the function term encapsulates side effects
F^{\prime}
and is itself pure,
marked with
\emptyset
. This is analogous to Graded Base, where coeffect grades annotate function types
(instead representing usage of the parameter).

Monads on the other hand provide a semantic technique for giving a
denotational model of various kinds of
effect
(
33
;
32
)
.
The
monadic meta language
provides a type constructor
T

where
T\tau
captures the type of computations performing some side
effects in order to compute a
\tau
value
(
33
)
.
Thus, terms of the monadic meta language have types
of the form:

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:T\tau\qquad\quad\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M^{\prime}:\sigma\rightarrow T\tau

On the right is an example of a function that encapsulates
side effects in its result, but the function itself does not need
wrapping in the monadic
T
constructor since abstraction is pure
(side-effect free). This is analogous to Linear Core if we had
only a single comonadic modality.

46
observed that the structure of effect systems
and the structure of the monadic meta language are the same
(
37
)
.
They thus married the two by annotating the monadic constructor
with effect information, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:T_{F}\tau\qquad\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda x.M^{\prime}:T_{\emptyset}(\sigma\rightarrow T_{F^{\prime}}\tau)

Later, this indexed structure was captured generally by the notion of

graded monads

(
26
)
. Graded
monads generalise monads to a family of constructors indexed by the
elements of a monoid, and whose operations are stratified and
accounted for by the monoidal structure. Graded monads thus unify
the semantics of effects with a syntactic analysis in the types.

Linear Core with graded modality
{\color[rgb]{0.0273,0.3125,0.8164}\square_{\mathit{r}}}\mathit{A}
is thus a dual
notion to the graded monadic calculus, unifying the semantics of
coeffects with a static analysis in the types, but with linearity
as its base typing system. Graded Base is analogous to effect systems. Indeed the early
work on coeffect systems (captured here as Graded Base) has no graded modality and was motivated
by dualising effect systems
(
39
;
40
)
.
As seen, without this graded modality, Graded Base cannot localise information
to sub-parts of complex types as discussed in Section
5
.

6.4.
Adjoint Relationship

45
follow
the Mixed Linear and Non-Linear logic approach of
(
4
)

to provide a pair of graded calculi with an adjunction mediating between them.
One calculus is a linear-base system with linear functions
and a mixed context of linear and graded assumptions. The other calculus
is a graded-base system but with no graded function arrow. A modality

\mathsf{Lin}
embeds formulae from the linear-base system into the graded-base. Conversely, a graded modality

\mathsf{Grd}_{r}
embeds formulae from the graded-base system into
the linear-base system, capturing their grade
r
and incurring a promotion (scaling of
the graded context). From pair
\mathsf{Lin}
and

\mathsf{Grd}_{r}
is derived a graded comonadic modality following the adjoint resolution of graded
comonads
(
14
)
.

The work seems related but on a more semantic level. Whilst we showed
that our translation is not adjoint in Section
2.3
, our two calculi have
function types on both sides which seemed to be the main blocker to forming an adjunction.
Further work is to ascertain whether our translation could
be restricted to the systems considered by
45
and then used
as a model of the adjunction by using our
translations as the semantics of the modalities operating on
syntactic categories of terms. Another avenue
would be to explore whether generalising function spaces on the graded-base
side would undermine their adjunction construction in general.

6.5.
Filling Out the Design Space: Cartesian Base

A central idea in this work has been to consider the ‘base’ notion of usage in
graded systems: linear-base systems have linear propositions, with a linear
axiom rule, and linear function type; graded-base systems have graded propositions,
with a graded axiom rule, and a graded function type. This framing suggest
an unexplored point in the design space: what about a graded system with
a cartesian base, i.e., with a cartesian axiom rule and standard cartesian
function (type), with contexts comprising both cartesian and graded
assumptions?

Crucially we must then consider how the cartesian part interacts with
the graded part. On the left, we have the rules for dereliction and
application in Linear Base, on the right have the potential rules
for a cartesian base. Since cartesian propositions can be used arbitrarily
we now require an element
\omega\in\mathcal{R}
of the semiring which
behaves in an absorbing fashion for both
+
and
\ast
.
Dereliction in cartesian base can then mark a cartesian assumption
with
\omega
. Application must also scale the argument context
\Gamma_{2}

by
\omega
since the function arrow is cartesian and so may use
its argument arbitrarily:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\text{(Linear Base)}\\[5.0pt]
\dfrac{\Gamma,x:A\vdash_{\textsc{l}}t:B}{\Gamma,x:[A]_{1}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}t:B}(der)\\[15.00002pt]
\dfrac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}t_{1}:A\multimap B\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}t_{2}:A}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}t_{1}\ t_{2}:B}(app)\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\text{(Cartesian Base)}\\[5.0pt]
\dfrac{\Gamma,x:A\vdash t:B}{\Gamma,x:[A]_{\omega}\vdash t:B}(der)\\[15.00002pt]
\dfrac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash t_{1}:A\rightarrow B\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{2}:A}{\Gamma_{1}+\omega\ast\Gamma_{2}\vdash t_{1}\ t_{2}:B}(app)\end{array}

The above seems a reasonable approach to building a
cartesian base. However, this system
provides little benefit as the grading quickly degenerates to
being always
\omega
in derivations. Consider below the derivation of
the ‘copy’ operation on an axiom to produce a pair:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\dfrac{\dfrac{x:A\vdash_{\textsc{l}}x:A}{x:[A]_{1}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}x:A}\quad\dfrac{x:A\vdash_{\textsc{l}}x:A}{x:[A]_{1}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}x:A}}{x:[A]_{1+1}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}(x,x):A\otimes A}&\dfrac{\dfrac{x:A\vdash x:A}{x:[A]_{\omega}\vdash x:A}\quad\dfrac{x:A\vdash x:A}{x:[A]_{\omega}\vdash x:A}}{x:[A]_{\omega}\vdash(x,x):A\otimes A}\end{array}

In Linear Base, there is a precise account of the non-linearity
incurred: the term uses
x
with graded
1+1
. However, in
Cartesian Base we can only say
\omega
due to the dereliction rule.
In the end, the Cartesian Base system
is ultimately no more informative as a graded system than
standard intuitionistic, cartesian systems.

6.6.
Resource-Aware Operational Semantics

The operational semantics presented here leverage standard
\lambda
-calculus CBN operational semantics,
which are typical for the literature studied.
More recent work has developed
resource-aware
operational
semantics
(
13
;
8
;
43
;
7
)
. These approaches are based on an abstract machine
approach with a variable environment, rather than a substitution-based model.
Conceptually, a resource-aware semantics keeps track not just of variable mappings in the evaluation environment,
but more broadly tracks resource supplies, which are consumed by variable use,
thereby making the connection between static and dynamic resource guarantees:
A
resource-sound
type system only accepts
well-resourced
programs,
whose evaluation under the resource-aware operational semantics respect the static resource bounds,
meaning (1) evaluation does not get stuck due to depletion (
resource progress
);
and (2) every evaluation step leads to a program with comparable resource requirements
under the (transitive) approximation relation (
\sqsubseteq
) of the resource algebra (
resource conservation
).

Most recently,
9
extend these guarantees to the converse property,
ensuring both upper and lower resource bounds, thereby also preventing unused resources (
wastefulness
).

Extending the various flavours of linear- and graded-base calculi and their translations,
which form the substrate of this paper, to such resource-aware operational semantics goes
beyond the scope of what we are able to investigate here. Instead, we leave this as future work.

6.7.
Conclusion

Our goal here has been to serve as a bridge between two different approaches
in the literature for constructing graded type systems that capture coeffects (context dependence).
We answer the long open question about the relationship between
the linear-base and graded-base styles and give a detailed analysis and metatheoretic results. Overall we see that we can translate between
the two flavours: with Linear Base and Graded Base having mutual embeddings
(but requiring a global CPS encoding for the Linear Base into Graded Base direction);
Linear Base and Graded Modal Base having (more straightforward) mutual embeddings;
and Linear Push Core and Graded Modal Core having mutual embeddings: these are the points
where the two styles of calculi can be inter-translated (see Figure
1
).

Now that graded types for tracking various kinds of program property are moving
from the laboratory to the field, with Haskell
(
5
)
, Idris
(
11
)
, Granule
(
38
)
, and lately OCaml with its moding system
(
29
;
17
)
,
our hope is that this study can serve as a design guide for
further theoretical work and for language designers wishing to integrate graded types
in some way, drawing from a wider arsenal of techniques and metatheoretic results.

Acknowledgments

We thank the anonymous reviewers of this work for their comments and feedback.
During its early stages, this work was partly supported by EPSRC grant EP/T013516/1.
Orchard also received support through Schmidt Sciences, LLC.

\end{array}}{[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{z}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}\;\triangleq\;\mathsf{case}\,[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{y^{\prime}}\rightarrow[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{y^{\prime}}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{z^{\prime}}\rightarrow[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{z^{\prime}}/\mathit{z}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\}}\;\textsc{{{substsume}}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{y^{\prime}},\mathit{z^{\prime}}\,\#\,\mathit{t}\\
\end{array}}{[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{y},\mathit{z}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;\triangleq\;\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{y^{\prime}},\mathit{z^{\prime}}\rangle=[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{z^{\prime}}/\mathit{z}][\mathit{y^{\prime}}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{{substprode}}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]

Typing

Linear Base typing plus the following:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{array}[]{c}{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{B}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}\;\textsc{{{$\otimes$-i${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}&\;\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:\mathit{A},\mathit{y}:\mathit{B}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\textsc{l}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{C}}\;\textsc{{{$\otimes$-e${}_{\textsf{L}}$}}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{(\mathsf{case}\,\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\})\equiv_{\textsc{g}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{x}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}-\oplus\beta 1}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{(\mathsf{case}\,\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\})\equiv_{\textsc{g}}[\mathit{t}/\mathit{y}]\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}-\oplus\beta 2}$}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{(\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{x};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{y}\})\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}-\oplus\eta}$}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}&\;\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{let}\,\langle\rangle=\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}1}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}&\;\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{(\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\equiv_{\textsc{g}}(\mathsf{let}\,\langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle=\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}})}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}\otimes{}E}$}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}&\;\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{\langle\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\langle\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}\otimes{}I}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{inj}_{1}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}\oplus{}I1}$}{}}}\quad{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathsf{inj}_{2}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}\oplus{}I2}$}{}}}\\[20.00003pt]
{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathit{t}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}}&\;\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\end{array}&\;\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\equiv_{\textsc{g}}\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\end{array}\\
\end{array}}{(\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\})\equiv_{\textsc{g}}(\mathsf{case}\,\mathit{t^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\mathsf{inj1}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\,\mathsf{inj2}\,\mathit{y}\rightarrow\mathit{t^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\})}\;\textsc{{$\equiv_{\textsc{G}_{\mathsf{cong}}\oplus{}E}$}{}}}\end{array}\end{aligned}

Proof of Soundness for Graded Base to Linear Base

2.3.1

B.1.1
,

,
and equation preservation in Appendix
